The promoter holding company, ADI BPO Services, has held exactly 68.34% in all twelve quarters. Foreign institutions fell from 3.71% to 1.49%, domestic institutions rose from 0.56% to 2.11%, the public holding went from 26.53% to 27.27%, and the number of shareholders rose from 15,737 to 26,333 (peaking at 29,852 in September 2025). The free float is about 31.7%.

Dilution is not a concern. The share count has been 17,105,816 since the FY22 buyback. FY21 to FY22 profit rose 47.5% while EPS rose 57.0% because shares fell 6%. The audited reconciliation shows no issue or buyback in FY25 or FY26 [1], [76]. Employee options are small and are met mainly from shares the MPS Employee Welfare Trust bought in the market. There are 158,316 options outstanding under the 2023 scheme (0.93% of shares) and 139,884 shares in the trust (0.82%). A fifth grant of 79,009 options was approved on 4 May 2026 (0.46%). Share-based pay expense was ₹1.04 Cr in FY26, 0.14% of revenue [1]. Chairman and CEO Rahul Arora’s remuneration rose 47% to ₹6.86 Cr (0.9% of revenue, 4.0% of profit). Related-party business is small: rent and infrastructure charges to the promoter and a KMP-controlled entity totalled ₹3.1 Cr (0.4% of revenue), and the security deposit with ADI BPO fell from ₹1.0 Cr to ₹0.83 Cr [1]. Several governance developments arrived through FY27: KPMG Assurance and Consulting Services LLP was appointed internal auditor for three years (FY27–FY29) alongside Walker Chandiok’s re-appointment for a second five-year statutory term; CRISIL ESG upgraded MPS’s unsolicited rating from 58 (Adequate) to 64 (Strong) on 4 September 2026; and an 8 August 2026 amendment to the MPS Interactive shareholders’ agreement merely allowed Rodney Charles Beach to transfer his minority stake to the Beach Trust without altering commercial terms or control (BSE/NSE filings, Jul–Sep 2026). The most important pending structural event remains the merger of promoter holding company ADI BPO Services Limited into MPS after demerger of its infrastructure-management and investing business. Shareholders approved it on 22 August 2026 (all 11,690,615 promoter votes, all institutional votes, and 703,540 of 703,590 other public votes in favour). The NCLT Chennai bench passed its second-motion order on 16 September 2026 and listed the hearing for 4 November 2026; through 25 November 2026 no final sanction order has been disclosed [1], [82], [83]. Crucially, inspection of the 5 August 2026 Addendum to the shareholders’ notice surfaces a key forensic caveat: D&A Financial Services (the SEBI-registered merchant banker certifying the abridged prospectus for ADI BPO) explicitly disclaimed auditing or reviewing ADI BPO’s financial statements or performing any financial, legal or tax due diligence, relying strictly on management representations, while the Addendum references pending Direct Tax (Income Tax) and Indirect Tax (GST) proceedings (MPS EGM Addendum, 5 Aug 2026). While cancellation and 1:1 re-issue of the 68.34% promoter block should not dilute public ownership, final NCLT sanction and confirmation that no residual ADI BPO tax liabilities pass onto MPS remain active watch items.

Top customer and top 15 customers, % of revenuen/a18.6% and 61.1%17.3% and 58.0%High concentration, easing

The auditor’s opinion has been unmodified in all three years, with no emphasis-of-matter paragraph. But every year’s key audit matters include acquisition accounting, and FY26 adds goodwill impairment. The same audit-trail exception has appeared for three consecutive years. The CARO schedule lists ₹36.3 Cr of disputed income-tax demands while the contingent-liability note shows ₹4.0 Cr. We could not reconcile the difference from the text.

A mentor’s reading of the audit pages is deliberately blunt. The positive side first. The audit opinion is clean and internal financial controls were reported effective. There were no going-concern doubts and no fraud reported in the CARO statement. There were no delays with statutory dues beyond ‘slight delays in a few cases’, and no covenant breach on the loan [1]. The negatives are pointed, not alarming. First, the audit-trail point. The proviso to Rule 3(1) of the Companies (Accounts) Rules requires accounting software with an edit log that cannot be disabled, for years beginning on or after 1 April 2023. Auditors report on five points under Rule 11(g) [84]. MPS’s auditor has reported the same exception in FY24, FY25 and FY26. The edit log is not enabled at database level in the main accounting software, and the third-party payroll provider’s assurance report is silent on it [15], [16], [1]. That is a systems fix, not an earnings issue. But three years without the fix is worth a line in the control-environment score. Second, the key audit matters. In each of three years the auditor has spent its most significant effort on purchase-price allocation, a subjective exercise in future cash flows, growth rates and discount rates. In FY26 it added the goodwill impairment test and noted a ₹12.9 Cr charge [1]. Third, the tax disputes. The CARO schedule lists income-tax demands totalling ₹36.3 Cr. Of these, ₹21.8 Cr relates to assessment year 2023-24 and sits before the Assessing Officer. The note on contingent liabilities shows only ₹4.0 Cr. The likeliest explanation is that most of the demand is already provided for or expected to be reversed, but the annual report does not say so [1].

Ten of the sixteen tests grade A- or better, or B+. The weaker six are acquisition accounting and goodwill headroom, the quality of other income and the exceptional layer, control-environment hygiene, and structural currency exposure. Our overall grade for earnings quality is B+. The cash is strong and the balance sheet clean, but a non-trivial layer of accounting is subjective.

Five accounting and cash-flow things that could be wrong
#What could be wrongEvidence in the recordWhat to watch
1Unbound’s purchase-price allocation and goodwill move, or retention disappoints₹123.9 Cr goodwill (88% of price) plus ₹52.3 Cr intangibles; provisional until Feb 2027; AJE goodwill was cut 28% in the same exerciseRestated Unbound goodwill in the FY27 report; renewal rates and subscription revenue; the amortisation charge on the ₹52.3 Cr of intangibles
2Corporate Learning needs a larger impairmentGoodwill ₹100.4 Cr against segment result of ₹11.3 Cr; discount rates 14.5% to 19%Segment result margin (11.8% in FY26) holding above the first-half FY27 level; any second impairment
3FY26 profit overstated by non-repeatable accounting gains₹29.7 Cr gross exceptional gains; ₹14.0 Cr customer-advance write-back; ₹6.4 Cr ordinary write-backsExceptional gains above 5% of pre-tax profit; write-backs above 25% of other income
4Acquired deferred revenue turns into revenue without new cash, or working-capital drains recurUnbound brought ₹35.0 Cr of contract liabilities; FY25’s ₹64.4 Cr working-capital drain followed an acquisition-heavy FY24 and is not itemisedContract liabilities excluding acquisition and currency (fell ₹17.1 Cr in FY26, including the ₹14.0 Cr write-back); CFO/EBITDA below 70% without an explanation
5Tax disputes and control gaps are larger than disclosed₹36.3 Cr of disputed income-tax demands against ₹4.0 Cr in the note; audit-trail exception for three yearsOutcome of the AY2023-24 and AY2024-25 appeals; removal of the audit-trail exception; liabilities inherited from the promoter holdco merger

None of these is a present misstatement. Each is a place where the audited numbers rest on a judgement or an accounting reversal. A bad outcome would show up first as a write-down or a cash-conversion miss, not as an operating miss.

What would change our view on earnings quality (business-quality triggers only). First, CFO below 70% of EBITDA for two consecutive years with no acquisition explanation. Second, a second goodwill impairment in Corporate Learning, or an impairment in Research Solutions. Third, net exceptional gains above 5% of pre-tax profit again. Fourth, bank debt above 0.5 times EBITDA for more than two quarters. Fifth, a restated Unbound allocation that cuts goodwill sharply or raises it materially. Sixth, removal of the audit-trail exception, which would be a favourable change. Seventh, a merger scheme that brings unexpected liabilities from the promoter holding company.

Our independent judgement. On the audited record this is a high-quality earnings stream with a layer of subjective accounting on top. The strong parts are verified. There are ten years of 1.00x cash conversion and capital expenditure near 1% of revenue. FY26 free cash flow after leases was ₹183.6 Cr. The share count has not changed since FY22. The loan was repaid within six months, and ₹163 Cr of cash sat against zero bank debt at September 2026. Receivable days fell to 38. The audit opinion is clean with effective internal financial controls [1], [2], [76]. The weak parts are also verified. Other income is lumpy and 41% write-backs. The exceptional layer helped FY26 by ₹7.6 Cr net (₹29.7 Cr gross). A policy change released ₹14.0 Cr of AJE customer advances. The tax rate was 1.5 points lower than FY25. Goodwill and acquired intangibles are 52.5% of assets, and the Corporate Learning unit carries 8.9 times segment result in goodwill. Three versions of ROCE are in circulation, and the same audit-trail exception has appeared for three years [1], [16], [15]. Net, reported FY26 profit of ₹173.2 Cr includes about ₹3.5 Cr from a lower tax rate and ₹7.6 Cr (pre-tax) from exceptional items. Strip the exceptional items, and separately the write-backs and fair-value gains in other income. Underlying pre-tax growth was then between 12.6% and 13.5%, not the 16.3% reported. That is still a good number. We rate earnings quality B+. On the evidence, the cash-flow statement is more reliable than the P&L for this company.

7. Why the Price Is What It Is: Valuation, Reverse DCF & Peer Comps

Core Investor Question: At ₹2,105.60 on the 23 November NSE ledger and ₹2,054.80 on the 25 November live TradingView close, what future cash-flow growth and terminal durability is the market pricing in, and how do our Pass 1 blind spots reshape fair value?

Chapters 1 to 6 judged the business without looking at a share quote. Now we put a price on it. Two distinct market prints govern this refresh, and we keep them strictly separate throughout. The verified NSE ledger close for 23 November 2026 is ₹2,105.60 [90]. Two sessions later, the live TradingView snapshot for 25 November 2026 shows a close of ₹2,054.80 (−2.41% on the day) [91]. Both sit well below the prior monograph close of ₹2,297.00 on 12 November 2026. The diluted share count remains 17,105,816 (1.7106 Cr), unchanged since the FY22 buyback [1]. Market capitalisation is therefore ₹3,601.8 Cr at the 23 November ledger close and ₹3,514.9 Cr at the 25 November live print (Screener rounds this to ₹3,515 Cr; TradingView displays ₹3,601.54 Cr using a slightly higher share base) [91], [93]. Cash stood at ₹163.23 Cr on 30 September 2026 with zero bank debt after the Unbound Medicine loan was repaid in August [2], [106]. Adding roughly ₹18 Cr of lease liabilities places enterprise value at ₹3,456.6 Cr on the 23 November ledger and ₹3,369.7 Cr on the 25 November live close.

The valuation dashboard immediately below compares every headline multiple across both session dates so readers can see how the two-day break below ₹2,085 compressed trailing and forward multiples.

On headline numbers, MPS looks optically cheaper than two weeks ago: 18.86x trailing earnings on TradingView, 16.7x refreshed FY27E earnings, and 10.8x forward EBITDA at the 25 November close of ₹2,054.80. A 6.3% forward cash-flow yield on a debt-free 35% to 39% ROCE business always catches the eye. Yet optical cheapness is never a buy signal by itself. Our Pass 1 refresh uncovered organic Education contraction, Unbound's royalty-capped margin and OpenEvidence rivalry, and a sharp Research hiring surge that explain why the market is still repricing terminal durability.

To place today's multiple in context, we map MPS's weekly trailing price-to-earnings ratio across a full decade. Each fiscal year's audited earnings per share enters the denominator from 1 June following year-end, so the band captures true historical investor moods across bull runs and drawdowns.

Even after sliding 31% off its August 52-week high, MPS at 20.3x FY26 EPS (25 November live print) and 20.8x (23 November ledger) still trades above its ten-year median of 17.6x. It has fallen below the five-year median of 22.7x, but it is nowhere near the 13.8x to 16.0x trough multiples seen during prior deratings. Multiple compression can travel further whenever growth doubts appear.

History proves how violently MPS's multiple swings even when underlying earnings keep climbing. The cycle log below links every major turning point since 2015 to the trailing multiple and business catalyst driving it.

Three drawdowns exceeding 50% since 2015 (−57.7%, −67.5%, and −51.9%) establish the true volatility base rate of this stock. In 2025 to 2026, trailing EPS grew from ₹69.43 to ₹87.05 and then ₹101.26, yet the multiple collapsed from 41.7x to 16.0x. The multiple, not next quarter's earnings, drives most of the drawdown risk.

The 2026 AI-disintermediation sell-off across global information and Indian BPM/software vendors explains the macro backdrop behind that multiple compression. We update the dated event log with the 25 November 2026 peer readings from our live TradingView snapshot.

MPS is no longer escaping the sector AI derating. Over three months to 25 November 2026, MPS fell 24.6%—matching eClerx (−24.5%) and trailing Newgen (−21.2%). Worse, the October 2026 Johns Hopkins collaboration with free clinical-AI leader OpenEvidence introduced a company-specific terminal-value threat to Unbound Medicine that was missing from our November 12 underwriting.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: a multiple is a mood about terminal value, not next quarter's EBITDA

When MPS raises its FY27 EBITDA floor to at least ₹310 Cr on 4 November and the stock still drops 11% over the next three weeks, the market is telling you something specific. Investors are not disputing H1 FY27's ₹154 Cr of banked EBITDA. They are questioning how much margin survives 2028 to 2032 once AI reprices production rates and free clinical AI challenges licensed reference apps.

Our response is disciplined arithmetic. First, solve for the free-cash-flow growth embedded in today's price via a reverse discounted cash flow. Second, test whether our newly audited Pass 1 facts make that implied growth easy or fragile.

We run a ten-year reverse discounted cash flow anchored on refreshed FY27E Base free cash flow to the firm of ₹212.0 Cr. That starting figure builds from ₹312 Cr EBITDA, less ₹36 Cr depreciation, less 25% tax on EBIT, plus ₹36 Cr depreciation, less ₹11 Cr capital expenditure, ₹5 Cr lease outflows, and a ₹15 Cr working-capital build. That equals a 6.1% yield on the 23 November enterprise value of ₹3,456.6 Cr and a 6.3% yield on the 25 November live enterprise value of ₹3,369.7 Cr. Free-cash-flow growth fades linearly over ten years from the solved year-one rate to the terminal rate.

The number:

At the 23 November ledger close of ₹2,105.60 (EV ₹3,457 Cr), a 13% hurdle rate and 5% terminal growth require 10.5% year-one free-cash-flow growth fading linearly to 5% (7.8% average over ten years). At the 25 November live close of ₹2,054.80 (EV ₹3,370 Cr), the requirement falls to 9.9% in year one (7.4% average).

How it was calculated:

Set enterprise value equal to the present value of ten years of free cash flow to the firm plus a Gordon-growth terminal value, then solve numerically for the year-one growth rate under linear fade to terminal growth.

The inputs:

Base FY27E FCFF of ₹212.0 Cr (EBITDA ₹312 Cr, D&A ₹36 Cr, tax 25% on EBIT, capex ₹11 Cr, leases ₹5 Cr, working-capital build ₹15 Cr), discount rate 13%, terminal growth 5%, and verified ledger EV ₹3,456.6 Cr (live TV EV ₹3,369.7 Cr).

The range:

At 12% and 5% terminal growth, ledger EV needs just 7.1% year-one growth. At 14% and 5%, it needs 13.7%. At 13% and 4%, it needs 12.9%. Across the full 11% to 15% discount-rate and 3% to 6% terminal-growth grid, required year-one growth spans −0.2% to 20.6%.

If the number is off:

If free cash flow compounds at a steady 8.0% for ten years and 5% thereafter, a buyer earns 13.0% a year from ₹2,105.60 and 13.2% from ₹2,054.80. If AI price give-back and Education erosion hold growth to 5.0%, the return slips to 11.4% to 11.6%—below our 13% small-cap hurdle.

Inside the realistic small-cap band of 12% to 14% hurdle rates and 4% to 5% terminal growth, the market requires 7.1% to 15.9% year-one free-cash-flow growth. The central cell (13% / 5%) asks for 10.5% in year one (9.9% at the 25 November live print)—almost identical to MPS's 10.2% eleven-year EBITDA CAGR. Bears sit in the 14% to 15% / 3% corner (17.9% to 20.6%), pricing weak terminal longevity.

On an acquisition-free cash-flow base (₹212.0 Cr FCFF), repeating MPS's 10.2% eleven-year EBITDA CAGR implies a 14.2% to 14.4% annual return. However, that baseline credits acquisition-fueled historical growth without deducting historical acquisition spend. Charging M&A reinvestment below cuts the implied return under our 13% hurdle.

M&A-Adjusted Owner FCFF Stress Audit (Red-Team Reinvestment Check). MPS's historical 10.2% eleven-year EBITDA CAGR and 12.7%/17.1% five-year CAGRs were not free organic growth. Per Chapter 1, four priced FY22–FY26 deals—EI Design (₹42.09 Cr), Liberate (₹70.33 Cr), AJE (₹69.67 Cr), and Unbound Medicine (₹140.40 Cr)—consumed ₹322.49 Cr (₹64.5 Cr per year), with AJE supplying ~92% of FY25 sales growth and Unbound supplying over 100% of H1 FY27 Education growth [1], [15], [102]. Separately, FY23–FY26 working-capital drag averaged ₹24 Cr per year (Chapter 6) against ₹15 Cr in our base FCFF. Deducting ₹64.5 Cr/yr of acquisition reinvestment lowers owner FCFF to ₹147.5 Cr (and ₹138.5 Cr if working capital normalizes to ₹24 Cr).

Once we stop treating acquisition-fueled growth as free, the long-term cash-flow case drops below our 13% small-cap hurdle. Deducting ₹64.5 Cr/yr of historical M&A reinvestment cuts owner FCFF to ₹147.5 Cr, lifts required Year-1 growth to 20.0% (ledger) / 19.3% (live TV), and compresses the 10.2%-growth IRR to 11.6%–11.7% (11.2%–11.4% at ₹24 Cr working-capital drag)—directly reinforcing our REJECTED verdict.

Why does the market refuse to pay more than 18.86x trailing earnings for a 38.7% ROCE franchise? Our Pass 1 blind-spot audit surfaced four concrete fundamental overhangs that sharpen the skepticism beyond generic AI fear. First, organic Education is contracting and Unbound is royalty-capped. Subtracting Unbound Medicine's disclosed Q2 revenue of ₹21.07 Cr and EBITDA of ₹3.82 Cr (18.13% margin; H1 ₹41.64 Cr revenue / ₹7.48 Cr EBITDA = 17.96%) from Education Solutions reveals that organic Education contracted −3.8% YoY in H1 and −9.9% YoY in Q2, while organic Q2 EBITDA fell −23.3% YoY (₹17.07 Cr vs ₹22.25 Cr) [102], [104]. Management's own Q2 presentation confirms Unbound's royalty-based third-party content model carries a structurally lower margin ceiling than MPS's solutions base [102]. Second, OpenEvidence directly threatens Unbound's moat. A Harvard Medical School study in JAMA (15 October 2026) showed OpenEvidence reaching 703,265 monthly active U.S. clinicians for free, and on 13 October 2026 Johns Hopkins Medicine—one of Unbound's flagship licensors—signed a landmark AI medical-education alliance with OpenEvidence while Wolters Kluwer's UpToDate Expert AI reached 2,500 U.S. hospitals [99], [100], [101]. Third, Research headcount jumped ahead of billing. Group headcount rose +11.16% YoY to 3,557 in Q2 FY27, driven by Research Solutions headcount surging +18.02% YoY to 2,666 (+20.42% ex-AJE)—nearly matching +21.71% ex-AJE revenue growth [103]. Until November–December mandate billing converts, the near-flat headcount AI-leverage story is unproven. Fourth, governance and concentration remain real discounts: one customer is 17.3% of revenue, goodwill plus acquired intangibles are 52.5% of assets, the Chief People Officer resigned inside four months amid 28.85% attrition, and merchant banker D&A Financial Services disclaimed independent financial, legal, or tax due diligence on the ADI BPO promoter merger despite pending tax proceedings [1], [107], [108].

For full session transparency, 23 November 2026 verified NSE ledger closes were ₹2,105.60 for MPS, ₹1,511.40 for eClerx, ₹283.60 for Firstsource, and ₹1,160.50 for Cyient (Newgen is priced from the 25 November live snapshot).

Severe-Bear / RWS-Style Stress Test (Red-Team Downside Audit). What if H2 FY27 Research billing fails to offset Q2's +20.42% ex-AJE hiring surge while organic Education remains negative—pulling FY27E EBITDA below the ₹310 Cr floor to ₹295.0 Cr (PAT ₹198.0 Cr, EPS ₹115.75)? And what if FY28E revenue stalls near annualized H1 (₹900 Cr) while AI renewal give-back and Unbound attrition compress consolidated EBITDA margin to 28.5% (just below FY25's 29.0% strong-form bear line from Chapter 4, echoing RWS Holdings' 600 bps reset) to yield ₹256.5 Cr EBITDA (PAT ₹167.6 Cr, EPS ₹97.99; Nov 2027 LTM EPS ₹106.87) [67]? Applying 12.5x—between MPS's 10th-percentile multiple (11.7x; Feb 2019 trough 11.5x) and Wolters Kluwer's 2026 buyback multiple (12.8x)—cuts Bear value to ₹1,336, matching MPS's March 2026 intraday low (₹1,336.10) [65], [89], [90].

The number:

Refreshed Base-case LTM EPS (Basis B) at November 2027 is ₹130.0 (average of FY27E ₹123.2 and FY28E ₹136.8). Applied at the ten-year median multiple of 17.5x, Base fair value is ₹2,275 per share.

How it was calculated:

Subtract ₹36 Cr D&A (FY27E) and ₹38 Cr D&A (FY28E) from Base EBITDA of ₹312 Cr and ₹345 Cr, add ₹8 Cr other income, deduct ₹3 Cr interest, apply a 25% tax rate, and divide by 1.7106 Cr shares.

The inputs:

FY27E EBITDA ₹312 Cr (above CFO Prarthana Agarwal's ₹310 Cr floor), FY28E EBITDA ₹345 Cr (+10.6% organic compounding with no assumed deal), 25% tax rate matching H1 FY27, and unchanged 1.7106 Cr shares.

The range:

Central Bear LTM EPS is ₹113.8 (₹1,593 at 14.0x), Severe-Bear LTM EPS is ₹106.87 (₹1,336 at 12.5x), and Bull LTM EPS is ₹146.7 (₹3,080 at 21.0x). Every ₹10 Cr swing in both years' EBITDA shifts LTM EPS by ₹4.38 (₹77 per share at 17.5x). Each single turn of P/E moves Base value by ₹130 per share (6.3% of the 25 November live price).

If the number is off:

Multiple sensitivity outweighs near-term EBITDA sensitivity. If Research billing conversion stalls in H2 FY27 and Education keeps shrinking, a 14.0x multiple on ₹113.8 EPS pulls the stock toward ₹1,593—and an RWS-style margin reset below 29% at 12.5x opens ₹1,336 (−35.0% to −36.6%). Conversely, proving organic Education recovery and 47%+ Research margins would justify lifting Base fair value back toward ₹2,500+.

Historical Cycle & Valuation Twin: Wolters Kluwer (information, software and clinical-reference platforms)

Netherlands, global; February to November 2026 AI-disruption derating

Shared Mechanism: A high-ROCE information franchise with 83% recurring revenue suffered a 47% average-price compression after Anthropic released its Claude legal plug-in on 3 February 2026 [65], [85]. Morningstar lowered Wolters Kluwer from wide moat to narrow moat on 5 March 2026 even though reported revenue and profit growth never slowed [85].

Historical Numbers & Outcome: FY25 organic revenue grew 6% to €6,125m at a 27.5% adjusted operating margin, diluted adjusted EPS rose 9% to €5.29, and 9M 2026 organic growth held at 6% with 10% operating-profit growth [65], [89]. Yet average share-buyback prices fell from €128.45 in 2025 (~24.3x) to €67.97 through 3 November 2026 (~12.8x)—a 47% derating while earnings rose [65], [89].

Direct Implication for MPSLTD: Quality earnings alone cannot arrest a terminal-value multiple reset. If Wolters Kluwer—with 83% recurring revenue and UpToDate Expert AI across 2,500 U.S. hospitals—compressed to ~13x earnings, MPS (with ~45% recurring revenue and Unbound now facing OpenEvidence at Johns Hopkins) cannot rely on valuation optics to put in a floor while its chart is breaking down.

Valuation upgrade and downgrade triggers. We lift fair value back above ₹2,400 only when four fundamental proofs arrive together: H2 FY27 EBITDA delivers at least ₹156 Cr (securing the ₹310 Cr floor), Research Solutions holds at least a 45% margin as November–December mandate billing scales revenue per employee, organic Education ex-Unbound returns to positive YoY revenue growth in H2 FY27, and Unbound Medicine proves institutional renewal resilience at or above 90% despite OpenEvidence. Conversely, we shift probability directly into the ₹1,593 Bear / ₹1,336 Severe-Bear band if Research margin slips below 42% for two quarters, organic Education stays negative through FY27, FY27 EBITDA guidance drops below ₹300 Cr, a goodwill write-down exceeds ₹25 Cr, or final ADI BPO sanction reveals adverse tax liabilities or public dilution.

Valuation therefore delivers a clear bridge to Chapter 8. At ₹2,105.60 (23 November ledger) and ₹2,054.80 (25 November live TradingView close), MPS is modestly cheaper than two weeks ago, yet our refreshed Pass 1 adjustments leave probability-weighted upside at just +6.0% to +8.6% (+2.3% to +4.8% under Severe-Bear ₹1,336 stress; +0.5% to +3.0% on strict year-end earnings) and cut M&A-adjusted ten-year IRR below our 13% hurdle to 11.6%–11.7%. There is no deep margin of safety to override a broken trend. Chapter 8 examines why the tape has now crossed decisively into Stage 4 and why catching a falling knife here violates our core risk discipline.

8. Stage 2 Technical Structure, Institutional Flow & Momentum Setup

Core Investor Question: Has MPSLTD broken decisively into a Stage 4 downtrend after losing its 200-day moving average, and what exact base-building and Stage 2 reclaim signals are required before committing capital?

The previous seven chapters judged the business and priced its cash flows. Now we inspect the tape, and the verdict is unmistakable: MPS has broken down into a confirmed Stage 4 decline. On our verified 23 November 2026 NSE ledger, MPS closed at ₹2,105.60 (−1.80% on the day, −9.00% over one month, −27.83% over three months), clinging just 1.14% above its 200-day simple moving average (₹2,081.77) and still reading Stage 3 on daily averages [90]. Two sessions later, on the 25 November 2026 live TradingView snapshot, sellers broke the floor cleanly: MPS closed at ₹2,054.80 (−2.41% on the day, −6.17% over one week, −24.56% over three months, −31.02% below its 52-week intraday high of ₹2,979.00) on 23,442 shares—1.47 times its 30-day average volume [91]. Every major weekly and daily moving average now sits above price, confirming a Stage 4 breakdown that blocks new equity capital.

This evaluation separates MPSLTD's medium-term trend from entry timing, so a screen signal does not become an unsupported order.

Technical structure confirms our fundamental caution. With the 200-day average broken on 1.47x volume and zero four-hour reversal, we reject new capital completely and keep passive reference markers strictly monitor-only until a Stage 1 base and high-volume Stage 2 reclaim above ₹2,293 appear.

Before reading the levels, recall Stan Weinstein's four-stage rule. Stage analysis uses the 30-week moving average as the primary referee: price above a rising 30-week line marks a Stage 2 advance; price churning sideways around a flattening line marks Stage 1 basing or Stage 3 topping; price sliced below the 30-week line alongside a falling 10-week average marks a Stage 4 decline [92]. Weinstein's core rule for disciplined capital is absolute: never catch a falling Stage 4 knife regardless of how attractive the trailing earnings multiple looks. Wait for selling pressure to exhaust itself, a Stage 1 base to form, and a high-volume Stage 2 reclaim to prove institutions are accumulating again [92]. Our 12 November monograph respected the boundary while MPS still sat 11% above its 200-day average. By 25 November, that last floor gave way.

NARRATOR'S REFLECTION: Why we cancel a watch when the floor breaks

Two weeks ago our plan looked neat on paper: wait for ₹2,070 at the rising 200-day average and buy a starter with a ₹2,005 stop. Today the stock reached ₹2,055—and instead of rushing to fill that old plan, we are rejecting it. That reversal is the heart of disciplined underwriting.

When price reaches your old support zone on 1.47x down-volume, slices cleanly beneath the 200-day average and pivot S1, and arrives alongside freshly uncovered Pass 1 cracks in organic Education and Unbound's Johns Hopkins licensor, the original thesis for a counter-trend bounce is void. You do not hold onto an old limit order after the floor gives way.

Our decision ladder maps the exact technical repair sequence required before MPS can ever leave our rejected file and return for re-underwriting.

Chapter 9 pulls Chapters 1 through 8 together into a unified, unbiased verdict across Bull, Bear, and three investment horizons, detailing our zero-allocation posture, monitor-only alerts, and where marginal Zerodha INR cash belongs instead.

9. Both Sides of the Aisle, Likely Outcomes & No-FOMO Action Plan

Core Investor Question: Weighing MPS's cash-rich Research franchise against Stage 4 technical breakdown and newly uncovered Pass 1 risks in Education, Unbound Medicine, and Research hiring, what is our unbiased verdict across three horizons and where should marginal cash sit?

The locked decision. Pass 1's Compounder Pass is strictly provisional: strong cash conversion and 35%–39% ROCE are tempered by a 37% FY26 drop in tangible net worth (₹102.2 Cr; goodwill+intangibles 52.5% of assets), 0% dividend yield, −9.9% Q2 organic Education contraction, ₹36.3 Cr unreconciled CARO tax disputes, and the ADI BPO due-diligence disclaimer [1], [104], [107]. Our November 2026 refresh cuts new capital from wait-for-support to REJECTED (unqualified tier, zero allocation). Our Zerodha account holds 0 shares of MPSLTD (not held; zero shares to sell), so this decision covers marginal INR cash only. Two forces drive the cut. First, the chart broke cleanly into Stage 4: MPS closed at ₹2,105.60 on 23 November before dropping −2.41% to ₹2,054.80 on 25 November (1.47x 30-day volume), breaking the 200-day average (₹2,084.67) and pivot S1 (₹2,065.93) [90], [91]. Second, Pass 1's bruises (organic Education −9.9%, OpenEvidence partnering with Unbound licensor Johns Hopkins Medicine, and +20.42% ex-AJE Research pre-hiring) leave just +6.0% to +8.6% base weighted upside (+2.3% to +4.8% under Severe-Bear ₹1,336 stress) and 11.6%–11.7% M&A-adjusted IRR [99], [103], [104]. We do not catch falling Stage 4 knives.

Neither session close offers enough reward for the risk. Even from the 25 November live print of ₹2,054.80, expected return is +8.6% on Basis B and +3.0% on strict Basis A. Both fall short of a 13% hurdle while Bear risk sits at −22.5%.

Splitting our call across three horizons explains why we reject new capital today without writing off MPS forever.

Short-term momentum is broken, and medium-term inflection is unproven. Long-term compounding faces new clinical-AI and Education tests. Across all actionable horizons, cash stays on the sidelines.

Every path back into MPS requires both chart repair and business proof. A lower price alone—even inside ₹1,810 to ₹1,920—never triggers a buy order without a Stage 1 base, Stage 2 reclaim, and H2 verification.

What could prove our rejection too cautious? First, MPS could be printing a washout low just under its 200-day average. If Q3 FY27 (due February 2027) delivers surging November–December peer-review billing, 48%+ Research margins, and an Education rebound, shares could snap back to ₹2,300–₹2,450 before a textbook Stage 1 base finishes. We gladly accept missing the first 10% to 15% of a V-shaped rally. At ₹2,350, today's ₹2,275 Base value leaves no margin of safety anyway. Paying for proof beats eating a Bear drop to ₹1,593 (−22.5% to −24.4%) or Severe-Bear stress to ₹1,336 (−35.0% to −36.6%). Second, management could deploy its ₹163.23 Cr cash into a high-margin proprietary platform deal at ~2x sales, bringing our 20% Bull case (₹3,080) into view. Even then, disciplined capital waits for real integration results and a rising 30-week trend.

NARRATOR'S REFLECTION: Rejecting a good company at the wrong moment is a win

Most mistakes start when an analyst falls in love with a clean ten-year cash statement and ignores a broken chart. MPS still earns 35% to 39% on capital and holds ₹163 Cr of debt-free cash. None of that vanished at ₹2,055.

Yet our job is to compound capital without eating avoidable 30% to 50% drawdowns. When the 200-day floor breaks on heavy volume in the same month organic Education drops and OpenEvidence signs Johns Hopkins, stepping aside is not fear. It is the discipline that keeps cash ready for confirmed Stage 2 leaders.
